1 Introduction

1.1 What we are solving

Every TLS connection we own agrees on a session key with a classical key exchange (X25519 or ECDHE on P-256) unless something changes it. Traffic recorded today can be decrypted once a large quantum computer exists ("harvest now, decrypt later"), so data that must stay confidential for years is exposed now, not later.

Three facts make this a programme rather than a setting:

  • Classical is still the default where we run. On AWS Lambda managed runtimes and Amazon Linux 2023 images, Python, Node.js and .NET negotiate classical X25519; Java before 25 and Node.js 18 and 20 cannot negotiate post-quantum at all. Only some newer images (Debian 13, Alpine 3.24, Ubuntu 26.04, official Node 22.20+ and 24.5+, Go 1.24+, JDK 25.0.5+) are post-quantum out of the box.
  • It fails silently. A hybrid client talking to a classical server, or a classical client talking to a hybrid server, falls back to classical with no error. Only logs, sensors and inventory show which connections are still classical.
  • One date is fixed. AWS blocks updates to deprecated Lambda runtimes (Node.js 18 and 20, Python 3.9 to 3.11, Java on Amazon Linux 2, provided.al2, dotnet8) from 2027-08-31. After that no layer, file or variable can be added to those functions.

1.2 What "PQC-enabled" means in this paper

Term Meaning How it is proven
PQ connection A TLS 1.3 handshake that negotiated a hybrid ML-KEM key exchange group: X25519MLKEM768 (or SecP256r1MLKEM768, SecP384r1MLKEM1024). ML-KEM is FIPS 203 The negotiated group in a log or sensor: CloudTrail tlsDetails.keyExchange, ALB/NLB logs, Zeek ssl.log curve, or the application's own handshake log
PQ-enabled client Offers a hybrid ML-KEM group first and still offers classical groups, so it connects to every server A PQ-only test server accepts it; a classical-only server still accepts it
PQ-enabled server or front door Accepts and prefers the hybrid group and still accepts classical clients A PQ client negotiates the hybrid group; a classical client still connects
PQ-done workload Every connection it owns that can be PQ is observed as PQ; the rest are recorded exceptions Dashboards (Inventory and dashboards), not the deployment record
PQ-signed certificate A certificate or signature that uses ML-DSA (FIPS 204). A separate track: see Use cases The certificate's algorithm; the TLS server_sigalg

What it does not mean:

  • Not new certificates. ML-KEM key exchange works with today's RSA and ECDSA certificates. ML-DSA certificates are a separate, slower track, by use case.
  • Not PQ-only. Every fix keeps classical groups as a fallback, so nothing breaks on the day it ships. PQ-only (TLS 1.3 with only hybrid groups) comes later, per endpoint, once logs show no classical traffic.
  • Not encryption at rest, application-layer cryptography or tokens. KMS keys, S3 encryption, JWT and message signing are out of scope, except where noted under code signing.
  • Not FIPS-validated PQ. FIPS workloads follow the FIPS notes per language and wait for validation evidence.

1.3 Scope: connections, compute types and languages

Connection Example Who is the TLS client Covered in
Internet to front door Browser or partner to ALB, NLB, CloudFront, API Gateway The caller Load balancers; Edge and API front doors
Front door to target ALB to an ECS service The load balancer Load balancers (target leg)
Workload to AWS APIs Lambda calling KMS, S3, Secrets Manager Our code (SDK) Solutions by compute type; by language; AWS service endpoints
Workload to internal services Service to service, private CA, mTLS Our code Solutions by compute type; by language; Service mesh
Workload to third parties SaaS APIs, partners, the internet Our code, or an egress proxy Solutions by language; Network devices and egress
Workload to data stores RDS, Aurora, ElastiCache, MSK, OpenSearch Our driver Databases and data services
Host administration SSH to EC2 OpenSSH Hosts and OS
Devices Sensors and gateways to AWS IoT Core Device firmware IoT
Certificates and signatures Server, client, user, device and code-signing certificates n/a Use cases; Certificates (ML-DSA)

Compute types: AWS Lambda (zip and container image), Amazon ECS on AWS Fargate, Amazon ECS on EC2 and Amazon EKS, and Amazon EC2 hosts. Languages: Go, Python, Node.js, Java and .NET, with notes on Rust, Ruby and PHP.

1.4 Approach

The programme runs in five steps, each with its own chapter. The language and its version decide whether a connection is PQ; the compute type decides how we see it and where the fix ships.

  1. Detect (chapter 3). Combine observed signals (what a handshake actually negotiated: CloudTrail, load balancer logs, Zeek, application logs) with posture signals (what a workload will negotiate, predicted from its runtime, versions and settings). Observed signals prove; posture covers what no log sees, such as Lambda and Fargate calls to non-AWS services.
  2. Inventory and track (chapter 4). Put every workload, front door, data service, host, device and certificate in one inventory, classify each against the matrices in this paper, and track the classical share on dashboards fed by the detection signals.
  3. Remediate (chapters 5, 6 and 7). Apply the tested fix for each language on each compute type, then the infrastructure services. Every fix is a file, a variable, a rebuild or a version bump, shipped through the shared build template and rolled back by removing it.
  4. Enforce (chapter 8). Guardrails in the pipeline, AWS Organizations and Kubernetes start in warn mode and then fail builds, so new work stays PQ.
  5. Prove (chapter 9). A workload is done only when the dashboards show its connections as PQ. Anything that cannot be PQ (blocked by AWS or a third party) is a recorded exception with an owner and a review date.

Certificates follow their own track alongside: ML-DSA roots and long-lived signatures first, internal dual-certificate pilots next, public HTTPS when AWS and browsers support it.

1.5 Summary of results

  • Every language can be made PQ on every compute type we run, with one change per workload. 437 client cases and 32 load balancer target cases pass end to end in LocalStack Pro on real Lambda, ECS and EC2 deployments (run 2026-11-16).
  • Certificates and keystores do not change for ML-KEM. Existing server certificates, private CA trust and mutual TLS keep working in all five languages.
  • ML-DSA certificates work today only on OpenSSL 3.5 stacks with ML-DSA enabled; Go, Java, OpenSSL 3.0 and the default Amazon Linux 2023 policy get ECDSA from a dual-certificate server. A dual-certificate server broke none of the 402 clients that connect at all.
  • Detection works from CloudTrail, load balancer logs, CloudWatch filters and Zeek (ssl.log field curve, tested with Zeek 7.2.2 and 8.0.10); Lambda and Fargate calls to non-AWS services remain posture-only.
  • AWS blocks some legs today: CloudFront to origin, API Gateway egress and HTTP/WebSocket APIs, IoT Core, RDS Proxy, ElastiCache, MSK, OpenSearch, Network Firewall TLS inspection and AWS VPN. These are recorded exceptions.
  • Not yet tested on real AWS. All tests ran on LocalStack and Docker on arm64; Phase 0 of the rollout plan closes that gap.

1.6 How to read this paper

Evidence labels used in every table:

Label Meaning
Tested Run between 2026-11-12 and 2026-11-16 in AWS Lambda base images, official language and distro images, or the LocalStack end-to-end suite (e2e/). Not run on real AWS
Documented Stated in vendor documentation, not tested here
Claimed Stated by a vendor as a plan or roadmap item, not yet shipped or confirmed
Unverified Believed true, no source opened or test run; confirm before relying on it

Code in this paper is limited to the main change. Every complete file (configs, IaC, scripts, policies) is in the repository under configs/, linked by relative path from each chapter and indexed in Appendix B. Test code is under e2e/ and tests/, listed per language in Appendix A. Paths are relative to the repository root.

2 Use cases: key exchange and certificates

This chapter says which use cases "PQC-enabled" covers, and which algorithm each one needs. The rest of the paper is mostly about the first column (key exchange), because that is where the risk is today and where every fix is available.

2.1 ML-KEM and ML-DSA

Two separate algorithms. ML-KEM (FIPS 203) protects key exchange: it stops traffic recorded today from being decrypted later, so it is urgent for every TLS connection, and it needs no new certificates. ML-DSA (FIPS 204) protects certificates and signatures: a forged signature only matters once a quantum computer exists, so it is urgent only where a signature or trust anchor must stay trusted for many years (device roots in firmware, code and firmware signing). ML-KEM cannot sign, so there is no "ML-KEM certificate".

"Hybrid" means different things on each side. For key exchange it is one group (X25519MLKEM768) that combines both algorithms, and it is what AWS, browsers and Go use. For TLS certificates, the deployable form today is a dual certificate: the server holds an ECDSA and an ML-DSA certificate and sends the one the client asks for (Tested, nginx and the e2e fixture). Composite ML-DSA certificates (one certificate, both algorithms) are standardized for X.509 by IETF LAMPS, but their TLS code points are still TBD in draft-ietf-tls-composite-mldsa-00 (October 2026), so no TLS stack negotiates them yet (Documented).

2.2 Use-case matrix

Use case Key exchange (ML-KEM) Certificate (ML-DSA) Evidence Risk and what to do
Public HTTPS (browsers, partners, public APIs) Hybrid X25519MLKEM768 at the ALB, NLB or CloudFront with a PQ security policy Not possible today. ACM, ALB, NLB, CloudFront and API Gateway serve RSA or ECDSA only; browsers do not negotiate ML-DSA server certificates, and Chrome plans Merkle Tree Certificates rather than ML-DSA X.509 Key exchange Tested (e2e LB cases) and Documented; certificate Documented Keep ECDSA certificates. Revisit when browsers and AWS certificate services ship a PQ format
Internal HTTPS (service to service, private CA) Hybrid X25519MLKEM768 in every language and compute type (this document) Dual certificate from a private CA (AWS Private CA issues ML-DSA). Clients on OpenSSL 3.5 get ML-DSA; Go and Java verify ML-DSA chains but negotiate ECDSA; OpenSSL 3.0 gets ECDSA. On AL2023 the crypto policy blocks ML-DSA until DEFAULT:PQ is set or SignatureAlgorithms is extended (configs/certificates-ml-dsa/openssl-pq-mldsa.cnf); our ML-KEM file alone does not Tested (e2e targets mldsa-dual, mldsa-only; per-stack table below) Pilot only. Never serve ML-DSA alone while any client is on Go, Java, OpenSSL 3.0 or an unmodified AL2023 policy: they fail the handshake. Chains grow from about 0.9 KB to 11.8 KB
Workload identity and mTLS (client certificates, IAM Roles Anywhere, mesh) Same as internal HTTPS Client certificates: tested per language with an ML-DSA client key (mldsa-mtls): works on OpenSSL 3.5 stacks with .NET 10, not .NET 8, Go or Java. IAM Roles Anywhere accepts ML-DSA trust anchors and end-entity certificates (AWS, February/March 2026). SPIRE and Envoy/BoringSSL: no upstream ML-DSA certificate support found Client side Tested; Roles Anywhere Documented; SPIRE and Envoy Unverified (not found) Roles Anywhere can pilot ML-DSA now with a Private CA trust anchor. Mesh and SPIFFE identities stay ECDSA until upstream support lands
User certificates (Windows logon, VPN, Wi-Fi, S/MIME) Windows Schannel hybrid ML-KEM (July 2026 update); VPN and Wi-Fi depend on the vendor AD CS on Windows Server 2025 issues ML-DSA (May 2026 update), with client-authentication templates for Windows 11 24H2/25H2. Microsoft calls Schannel ML-DSA a pilot, "not for production" (2026-10-08). Smart card logon: not confirmed. S/MIME: RFC 9882 defines ML-DSA in CMS; mail client support not found Documented (Microsoft); smart card and S/MIME clients Unverified Build a separate ML-DSA test CA (AD CS cannot convert an existing CA). Keep production user certificates ECDSA or RSA
Device and IoT certificates AWS IoT Core has no PQ policy; device legs stay classical (see IoT chapter) AWS IoT Core documents RSA and ECDSA device certificates only; no ML-DSA support found. Devices that ship a root in firmware for 10+ years need a PQ root before they ship IoT Core Documented (no PQ); ML-DSA Unverified Plan now for long-lived devices: put an ML-DSA root (alongside the ECDSA root) in new firmware and choose TLS stacks that can verify ML-DSA (OpenSSL 3.5, wolfSSL 5.8+)
Code and firmware signing Not TLS AWS KMS has ML-DSA keys (ML_DSA_44/65/87) and AWS Private CA issues ML-DSA code-signing certificates. AWS Signer (Lambda zips and container images) is ECDSA only Documented (AWS) Plan now for firmware and artifacts verified for years: sign with ML-DSA from KMS in the pipeline next to the existing signature; keep AWS Signer for Lambda code-signing config
SSH (hosts, users, bastions) mlkem768x25519-sha256 is the default in OpenSSH 10 (Tested) OpenSSH 10.6 (2026-10-06) added the hybrid ssh-mldsa44-ed25519 key type; 10.6p2 allows it as a CA signature algorithm. Older OpenSSH cannot use these keys Key exchange Tested; ML-DSA keys Documented (release notes), not tested here Upgrade OpenSSH for key exchange now. Pilot ML-DSA host and user keys only where every client is 10.6+
AWS API calls (SDK to AWS endpoints) Hybrid ML-KEM per language (this document) AWS's own certificates; nothing for us to change Tested None beyond the key-exchange fix

2.3 ML-DSA per client stack

ML-DSA per language (e2e, three targets on one fixture: mldsa-dual = dual certificate, mldsa-only = ML-DSA certificate only, mldsa-mtls = ML-DSA client certificate required).

Runs r1117034807 and r1117041043 (2026-11-16, identical ML-DSA results): 409 client cases across Lambda, ECS Fargate, ECS on EC2 and EC2 (the 28 pq-egress extension cases have no ML-DSA targets). 91 got ML-DSA on all three targets, 4 (.NET 8) on two, 307 got the ECDSA certificate from the dual server and failed the other two, and 7 failed every target for unrelated reasons (FIPS-only Go, a boringcrypto panic, Java 8 without the host-name flag, the shim without idna). Expectations per case: e2e/cases/mldsa_rules.py; the fixture: e2e/mldsa/server.py.

Client stack Dual server gives ML-DSA only ML-DSA client certificate Evidence
Node.js with bundled OpenSSL 3.5: Lambda nodejs22.x and nodejs24.x (with or without our fix), official images and binaries 22.20+ and 24.5+ ML-DSA Connects Works Tested (e2e)
Python, Node.js, .NET 10 on a distro OpenSSL 3.5: Debian 13, Alpine, Ubuntu 26.04 ML-DSA Connects Works Tested (e2e)
.NET 8 on any OpenSSL 3.5 base ML-DSA Connects Fails: .NET 8 cannot load an ML-DSA private key ("not a known key algorithm"); .NET 10 can Tested (e2e)
Amazon Linux 2023, default crypto policy: Lambda python3.12–3.14, dotnet8, dotnet10; AL2023 images and hosts ECDSA Fails Fails Tested (e2e)
AL2023 with our ML-KEM file openssl-pq.cnf (Python, .NET; it includes the policy's signature list) ECDSA Fails Fails Tested (e2e)
AL2023 with openssl-pq-mldsa.cnf ML-DSA Connects Works Tested (Python 3.12, outside the suite)
AL2023 with update-crypto-policies --set DEFAULT:PQ (image or host) ML-DSA Connects Works (.NET 8: fails, as above) Tested (e2e)
AL2023 Node.js from dnf with openssl-pq-node-system.cnf ML-DSA Connects Works Tested (e2e)
Lambda python3.9–3.11 with the pq-tls shim or layer (requests goes through pyOpenSSL, whose wheel bundles OpenSSL 3.5) ML-DSA Connects Works Tested (e2e); stdlib ssl on the same runtime stays ECDSA
OpenSSL 1.0.2, 1.1.1, 3.0 to 3.4: Node 18 and 20, Node 22.19 and 24.4, Debian 12, Ubuntu 24.04, Azure Linux 3.0, Amazon Linux 2 ECDSA Fails Fails Tested (e2e)
Go 1.23 to 1.27 (every compute type and FIPS mode that connects) ECDSA Fails Fails Tested (e2e); Go 1.27 verifies ML-DSA chains offline but crypto/tls does not negotiate ML-DSA
Java 8 to 25, SunJSSE and Bouncy Castle JSSE (kit v1 and v2) ECDSA Fails Fails Tested (e2e); JDK 25 PKIX validates ML-DSA chains offline
Any stack capped at TLS 1.2 (--tls-max-v1.2, EnabledSslProtocols=Tls12) ECDSA Fails Fails Tested (e2e); ML-DSA is TLS 1.3 only

Trust bundles. Every ML-DSA target used a bundle holding both the classical and the ML-DSA root (ca-both.crt, truststore-both.p12 for Java). All 402 clients that connect at all connected to the dual server, including OpenSSL 1.0.2, 1.1.1 and 3.0, Go 1.23 to 1.25 (which cannot parse ML-DSA) and Java 8 (Tested). So the ML-DSA root can be distributed ahead of any ML-DSA server certificate.

3 Detection

Detection answers two questions for every connection we own: is it PQ today, and did our fix make it PQ. No single signal answers both for every compute type, so we combine two kinds:

  • Observed: what a handshake actually negotiated, from an AWS log, a sensor or the application's own log. Observed signals prove a connection is PQ and catch what posture misses, such as a library that pins its own groups.
  • Posture: what a workload will negotiate, predicted from its runtime, versions and settings. Posture covers everything we can inventory, including Lambda and Fargate calls to non-AWS services, which no AWS log or sensor sees.

One rule holds for every source: a TLS 1.2 connection is never PQ, so the logged protocol version alone is enough to flag it.

3.1 Signals at a glance

Signal What it records Covers Evidence Configuration
CloudTrail tlsDetails.keyExchange Negotiated group per AWS API call Calls to KMS, STS, IAM, Secrets Manager, ACM, Roles Anywhere, Payment Cryptography (others "coming soon") Documented; the query and alarm are Unverified (no AWS account) cloudtrail-alarm.yaml, cloudtrail-non-pq.sql
ALB connection logs, NLB TLS access logs tls_keyexchange per inbound connection Every ALB and NLB TLS listener (logs are off by default) Documented elb-logs-athena.sql
CloudFront CloudFront-Viewer-TLS-Details header Viewer key exchange, passed to the origin CloudFront viewers Documented Edge and API front doors
Zeek on VPC Traffic Mirroring ssl.log field curve per session; our script adds pq and raises a notice East-west and egress traffic of Nitro EC2 ENIs: hosts, ECS and EKS on EC2, NAT instances, proxies Tested (Zeek 7.2.2 in the e2e stack; 8.0.10 on a pcap) pq-tls.zeek, traffic-mirror.tf
Application handshake log One JSON line per outbound handshake (event=tls_handshake, group, pq_status) Any compute type, including Lambda and Fargate, for code we own Tested (CloudWatch filter found 892 of 892 classical handshakes in run r1117041043) Per language chapter, "How to test"
Proxy and server logs Envoy %DOWNSTREAM_TLS_GROUP%, nginx $ssl_curve, HAProxy %[ssl_fc_curve] Self-managed web servers, proxies and egress gateways Tested (Envoy, nginx) Web servers and proxies, Network devices and egress
Posture inventory Runtime, versions, settings, policies per component, classified Everything the AWS APIs list Tested on LocalStack (25 of 25 components) Inventory and dashboards

3.2 Observed and posture signals by compute type

Observed signals by compute type

Compute type Calls to AWS APIs Calls to other services (internal, SaaS, internet) Inbound traffic Packet capture possible?
Lambda CloudTrail tlsDetails.keyExchange per call, for KMS, STS, IAM, Secrets Manager and ACM only; other services log version and cipher (Documented) No AWS log shows the group. Options: route VPC-attached egress through a sensor appliance, or log from the client library Front door logs (row below) No
Fargate (ECS or EKS) CloudTrail tlsDetails.keyExchange Same gap as Lambda; an egress proxy or sensor appliance in the VPC path Front door logs No. VPC Traffic Mirroring does not support Fargate (Documented)
Containers on EC2 (ECS, EKS, self-managed) and plain EC2 CloudTrail tlsDetails.keyExchange Zeek 7.2+ on VPC Traffic Mirroring logs the negotiated group in ssl.log field curve. Node-level eBPF agents are possible but young Front door logs plus mirroring Yes, Nitro instance ENIs
Front doors: ALB, NLB, CloudFront, API Gateway n/a n/a ALB connection logs and NLB access logs record the key exchange (Documented). CloudFront passes CloudFront-Viewer-TLS-Details to the origin, including the key exchange (Documented) n/a
IoT devices on AWS IoT Core n/a n/a IoT Core has no PQ security policy, so every device connection is classical today (Documented). Inventory devices by firmware and TLS library n/a

Posture signals by compute type

Compute type Inventory source What to read
Lambda lambda list-functions across accounts, or AWS Config Runtime identifier, PackageType (zip or image), OPENSSL_CONF, JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS, GODEBUG, attached layers. Classify with the matrix in the next section
Fargate and EC2 containers ECR images, task definitions, pod specs; an SBOM per image (Amazon Inspector SBOM export, Unverified) OS OpenSSL version, Go toolchain and go.mod version (go version -m <binary>), JDK version, Node version, Python's linked OpenSSL, env vars in the task definition
EC2 hosts SSM Inventory OS and OpenSSL version, crypto policy (update-crypto-policies --show)
