7 Infrastructure and services

Most infrastructure can be made PQ by configuration you control today; a few legs are blocked until AWS ships support. Each section that follows covers one area: what decides PQ, status, the server-side change, verification, pitfalls and the configuration files.

Where an area's client is our own code (an SDK calling AWS, a database driver, an app behind a mesh), the fix is the language fix: those sections point to Solutions by language and Solutions by compute type rather than repeat it, and add only what is specific to the service.

7.1 Areas at a glance

Area PQ today Main action Blocked by AWS Risk
Load balancers ALB and NLB opt-in; Classic none Set the 2025-09 PQ policy; SCP on elasticloadbalancing:SecurityPolicy (CreateListener, ModifyListener) Classic LB (migrate) IaC defaults to the classical 2016-08 policy; Security Hub ELB.17 passes non-PQ policies; the target leg follows the target's own TLS (Tested, 32 e2e cases)
Edge and API front doors CloudFront viewer on; API Gateway REST opt-in (TLS 1.2+1.3 only); Transfer Family SFTP on 2025-03 policies Set REST PQ policies; front HTTP and WebSocket APIs with CloudFront or a PQ ALB CloudFront origin leg, API Gateway egress, HTTP and WebSocket APIs Fronting HTTP and WebSocket APIs adds a hop, cost and a failure point; origin legs stay classical
AWS service endpoints KMS, Secrets Manager, STS, IAM, ACM, S3, DynamoDB on; others control plane or unlisted Client-side fixes only (language runbooks); CloudTrail keyExchange for detection SSM and other unlisted services Only CloudTrail-logged calls can be proven PQ; the rest rely on posture
Network devices and egress AWS network services undocumented; Squid, Envoy, strongSwan 6.0.2+ and vendor appliances ready Use a PQ egress proxy for what can't upgrade; Zeek for detection Network Firewall TLS inspection, AWS VPN TLS inspection re-terminates connections, so inspected traffic is classical; AWS VPN has no PQ option
IoT Classical into IoT Core; wolfSSL, awscrt and Mosquitto on OpenSSL 3.5 can do PQ Inventory TLS stacks; PQ-capable stacks for new devices; PQ gateways and brokers IoT Core (no PQ policy) Devices live 10+ years: the TLS stack and roots in firmware shipped now decide PQ for a decade; IoT Core has no ML-DSA either
Service mesh Linkerd 2.19+ on; Istio 1.27+, Consul 2.1+, Envoy opt-in Upgrade or set mesh config (Istio COMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc is PQ-only) ECS Service Connect (no setting) Istio COMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc is PQ-only and cuts off classical peers; ECS Service Connect has no setting
Databases and data services RDS and Aurora opt-in by version; PG18, MySQL 26.7+, Valkey 8.1 self-managed Engine upgrade, ssl_groups or group config, PQ-capable drivers RDS Proxy, ElastiCache, MSK, OpenSearch Engine and driver must both support the group; engine upgrades need maintenance windows; managed proxies and caches stay classical
Hosts and OS AL2023 and RHEL opt-in; Debian 13 and Ubuntu 26.04 on for OpenSSL programs only (not GnuTLS); Windows opt-in DEFAULT:PQ via SSM; Schannel group via GPO or SSM Amazon Linux 2 (migrate) Host-wide DEFAULT:PQ changes every OpenSSL program at once; GnuTLS tools (wget, git on Debian) stay classical; AL2023 also needs SignatureAlgorithms for ML-DSA
Certificates (ML-DSA) Private CA, KMS, Roles Anywhere can issue ML-DSA CLM pilot on internal mutual TLS after ML-KEM ACM, ELB, CloudFront, public web PKI An ML-DSA-only server breaks Go, Java, OpenSSL 3.0 and default AL2023 clients; chains grow 0.9 KB to 11.8 KB; composite certificates have no TLS code points yet
WAF AWS WAF never terminates TLS: the attached resource decides Set PQ on the resource; reverse-proxy WAFs need their own PQ profile Same as the resource it protects A WAF on a classical listener looks protected but is not PQ
Web servers and proxies OpenSSL 3.5, Go 1.24+ and JDK 25 servers PQ with one or two lines; Envoy and AL2023 builds classical until set Set the group list on both the listener and the upstream leg None (self-managed) Setting only the listener leaves the upstream leg classical; Envoy before 1.40 is classical unless ecdh_curves is set
Mobile, desktop and CLI clients Chrome, Edge, Firefox, Safari and Apple 26 OS stacks PQ; Windows Schannel opt-in; Android platform TLS not confirmed; macOS LibreSSL curl and GnuTLS tools not Upgrade CI toolboxes and clients; PQ only shows once the server offers it None (client-side) macOS LibreSSL curl and Debian GnuTLS tools stay classical; Android platform TLS not confirmed

7.2 Inbound: front doors and TLS 1.3 enforcement

Inbound connections become PQ when the front door's TLS policy offers ML-KEM and the caller supports it. Allow TLS 1.2 and 1.3 first, measure, then move each endpoint to TLS 1.3 only once its TLS 1.2 traffic reaches zero. TLS negotiation already picks 1.3 when both sides support it, and TLS 1.3's downgrade protection stops an attacker forcing 1.2.

Front door PQ setting Notes Evidence
ALB, NLB ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ-2025-09 (allows 1.2 and 1.3), later ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-3-PQ-2025-09 (1.3 only); FIPS variants exist Console default is a PQ policy, but CLI, CloudFormation and CDK default to ELBSecurityPolicy-2016-08. The backend leg also becomes PQ-capable: AWS then uses ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-0-PQ-2025-09 towards HTTPS targets, and the hop is PQ only when the target has its language fix (e2e target-leg cases, 2026-11-15; LocalStack can't test the listener leg itself). Enforce in an SCP with the elasticloadbalancing:SecurityPolicy condition key, which applies to CreateListener and ModifyListener (not CreateLoadBalancer) Documented
CloudFront Viewer side PQ by default on all policies Origin leg is classical; AWS says PQ is coming Documented
API Gateway REST (Regional, private) SecurityPolicy_TLS13_1_2_PQ_2025_09 and variants No TLS 1.3-only PQ policy exists. API Gateway egress to integrations is TLS 1.2 only, so that leg can never be PQ Documented
API Gateway HTTP, WebSocket, edge-optimized No PQ policy Front with CloudFront or ALB if PQ is required Documented (absence)
AWS IoT Core No PQ policy Every device leg classical until AWS adds one Documented (absence)
Service mesh (EKS) Linkerd 2.19+ PQ by default; Istio 1.27+ COMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc (enforces PQ, breaks non-PQ peers) Covers mesh-owned legs only, not app-originated TLS Documented

Move legacy callers that can't do TLS 1.3 to a separate listener or hostname that still allows 1.2, tracked as exceptions, rather than holding the main endpoint open for them. The SCP and Config rules that hold this line are on the Enforcement and guardrails tab. Check exact policy names with aws elbv2 describe-ssl-policies before putting them in templates.

7.3 Exceptions

Record every "blocked by AWS" leg in the exception register and recheck it against AWS's PQ readiness page each quarter.

7.4 Load balancers: ALB, NLB, GWLB, Classic

What decides PQ here

The load balancer's security policy decides the client-facing leg. Only policies with PQ in the name offer X25519MLKEM768, SecP256r1MLKEM768 and SecP384r1MLKEM1024, and only on TLS 1.3. The console defaults to a PQ policy; the CLI, CloudFormation, CDK, Terraform and the AWS Load Balancer Controller default to ELBSecurityPolicy-2016-08, which is classical. On the target leg the load balancer is the TLS client: AWS picks its backend policy from your listener policy, and the target's own TLS stack decides whether that hop is PQ. Sources: ALB policies, NLB policies (Documented, read 2026-11-14).

Status matrix

Load balancer / listenerDevice typeClient leg PQTarget legDefault from IaCEvidence
ALB, HTTPS listenerManaged AWS serviceOpt-in PQ policyBackend policy ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-0-PQ-2025-09 when any listener is PQ; PQ only if the target offers ML-KEMELBSecurityPolicy-2016-08 (classical)Documented
NLB, TLS listenerManaged AWS serviceOpt-in PQ policySame backend rule for TLS target groups; plaintext for TCP target groupsELBSecurityPolicy-2016-08Documented
NLB, TCP or UDP listenerManaged AWS servicePass-through: client and target negotiate end to endSame connectionnoneDocumented
Gateway Load BalancerManaged AWS service, GENEVE to appliancesNo TLS listener: end to end between client and server. An inspecting appliance behind it uses its own TLS stack, see Network devices and egressnonenoneDocumented (no TLS listener type)
Classic Load BalancerManaged AWS service (legacy)No PQ policy; migrate to ALB or NLBClassicalnoneDocumented
ALB or NLB via AWS Load Balancer Controller (EKS)Kubernetes controllerAnnotation or IngressClassParamsbackend-protocol annotationELBSecurityPolicy-2016-08 unless Helm defaultSSLPolicy is setDocumented (LBC annotations)
Lightsail load balancerManaged (Lightsail)No: only TLS-2016-08 and TLS-FS-1-2-Res-2019-08ClassicalTLS-2016-08Documented (Lightsail TLS policies)
Elastic BeanstalkManaged, creates an ALB/NLBThe policy you set on its listenerAs ALB/NLBPlatform defaultUnverified

PQ policy names, each prefixed ELBSecurityPolicy- and suffixed -2025-09: TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ (AWS's recommendation), TLS13-1-2-PQ, TLS13-1-2-Ext1-PQ, TLS13-1-2-Ext2-PQ, TLS13-1-3-PQ. FIPS: TLS13-1-2-Res-FIPS-PQ, TLS13-1-2-FIPS-PQ, TLS13-1-2-Ext0-FIPS-PQ, TLS13-1-2-Ext1-FIPS-PQ, TLS13-1-2-Ext2-FIPS-PQ, TLS13-1-3-FIPS-PQ. Backend only: TLS13-1-0-PQ, TLS13-1-0-FIPS-PQ. Documented. Confirm in your Region with aws elbv2 describe-ssl-policies.

Client side: the load balancer's connection to targets

The load balancer is the TLS client on the target leg, and you cannot choose its policy. AWS uses ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-0-PQ-2025-09 if any listener uses a PQ policy, ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-0-FIPS-PQ-2025-09 for a FIPS PQ listener, and an RFC 9151 listener overrides both (Documented). The backend policy offers ML-KEM groups, so the hop is PQ only when the target accepts one. AWS does not log the group negotiated to the target.

Target typeWhat the target must run for a PQ hopEvidence
ALB HTTPS target group (EC2, ECS, EKS pods)A TLS 1.3 server whose library offers X25519MLKEM768: nginx, Apache, HAProxy or Envoy on OpenSSL 3.5+ (Debian 13, Alpine 3.24), Go 1.24+, Java 25, .NET on OpenSSL 3.5. Server configs are in Web servers and proxies; runtimes in the language chaptersDocumented (policy); servers Tested in other chapters
ALB HTTP target groupPlaintext inside the VPC: no TLS on this hopDocumented
ALB Lambda targetInvoked by the service; not a TLS connection you configureDocumented
NLB TLS target groupSame as ALB HTTPS targetsDocumented
NLB TCP target group behind a TLS listenerPlaintext to the targetDocumented
NLB TCP listener (pass-through)The target is the TLS server for the client; its stack decides PQ end to endDocumented

To see the group on the target leg, log it on the target (for nginx, the $ssl_curve variable; see Web servers and proxies). The target sees the load balancer as its client, not the end user.

Target leg tested on LocalStack (2026-11-15, model of the load balancer's client). LocalStack's ALB ignores listener security policies (an ELBSecurityPolicy-2016-08 listener still negotiated X25519MLKEM768 with LocalStack's own certificate), so the listener leg needs real AWS. The target leg was tested instead: each workload ran as the HTTPS target on ECS Fargate or ECS on EC2 with its existing RSA certificate, and the e2e observer connected to it twice, once offering what AWS documents for ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-0-PQ-2025-09 (TLS 1.0 to 1.3, the three ML-KEM hybrids plus classical groups) and once as ELBSecurityPolicy-2016-08 (TLS 1.0 to 1.2), without verifying the certificate, as the load balancer does. The certificate never changed. 32 cases, all as expected (e2e/cases/server.py, cases srv-*):

Target workloadNo fixWith the language chapter's fixPQ with no change
Python (ssl module server)AL2023 Python 3.12, python:3.12-slim-bookworm: classicalOPENSSL_CONF or DEFAULT:PQ: X25519MLKEM768python:3.13-slim-trixie
Node.js (https.createServer)node:20-slim, AL2023 nodejs22: classicalAL2023 nodejs20/22 + OPENSSL_CONF or DEFAULT:PQ: X25519MLKEM768node:24-slim
Go (net/http)Go 1.23; Go 1.26 with go 1.23: classicalGODEBUG=tlsmlkem=1: X25519MLKEM768Go 1.26, go 1.26
Java (HttpsServer)Corretto 8, 21: classicalKit v2 on 8, 17, 21: X25519MLKEM768Corretto 25.0.5
.NET (SslStream, Kestrel's TLS layer)runtime:8.0 (Debian 12), AL2023 .NET 8: classicalAL2023 + OPENSSL_CONF or DEFAULT:PQ: X25519MLKEM76810.0-alpine, aspnet:10.0-resolute

Every target negotiated classical TLS 1.2 under the non-PQ backend policy, fixed or not, so moving the listener to a PQ policy is required for a PQ target leg, and the target's own fix is required as well. This models the load balancer's ClientHello with Go's TLS client, not AWS's s2n-tls; confirm on real AWS by logging the negotiated group on the target.

Server side: the listener

ToolSettingEvidence

Terraform:

resource "aws_lb_listener" "https" {
  load_balancer_arn = aws_lb.alb.arn
  port              = 443
  protocol          = "HTTPS"
  ssl_policy        = "ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ-2025-09"
  certificate_arn   = var.certificate_arn
  default_action {
    type             = "forward"
    target_group_arn = aws_lb_target_group.app_https.arn
  }
}

CloudFormation:

HttpsListener:
  Type: AWS::ElasticLoadBalancingV2::Listener
  Properties:
    LoadBalancerArn: !Ref Alb
    Port: 443
    Protocol: HTTPS
    SslPolicy: ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ-2025-09
    Certificates: [{CertificateArn: !Ref CertificateArn}]
    DefaultActions: [{Type: forward, TargetGroupArn: !Ref AppTargetGroup}]

CDK (TypeScript, not compiled here):

alb.addListener('Https', {
  port: 443,
  certificates: [cert],
  sslPolicy: elbv2.SslPolicy.TLS13_12_RES_PQ,
  defaultTargetGroups: [tg],
});

EKS annotations (ALB Ingress, then NLB Service):

alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/ssl-policy: ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ-2025-09
alb.ingress.kubernetes.io/backend-protocol: HTTPS
---
service.beta.kubernetes.io/aws-load-balancer-ssl-negotiation-policy: ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ-2025-09
service.beta.kubernetes.io/aws-load-balancer-backend-protocol: ssl

mTLS on ALB. Mutual authentication (verify or passthrough) is a listener setting next to the policy, so a PQ policy and mTLS sit on the same listener. The client certificate's signature algorithm is separate from the key exchange. The connection log code ClientCertIncompatible means the client certificate does not fit the chosen policy (connection logs, Documented). A PQ plus mTLS handshake was not tested here.

Packaging and rollout from TLS 1.2+1.3 to 1.3-only

PlatformWhere the policy livesFile (below)
Terraformaws_lb_listener.ssl_policy, connection and access logs on aws_lbiac/lb/main.tf
CloudFormationSslPolicy parameter with AllowedValues limited to PQ policiesiac/lb/lb.yaml
EKSIngressClassParams.spec.sslPolicy per ALB class, Service annotation for NLB, Helm defaultSSLPolicy as the cluster defaultiac/lb/k8s-lbc.yaml
Organisation guardrailSCP on elasticloadbalancing:SecurityPolicyiac/lb/scp-elb-pq.json
DetectionAthena tables and queriesiac/lb/athena.sql

  1. Turn on ALB connection logs and NLB access logs (both off by default) and create the Athena tables.

  2. Move every HTTPS and TLS listener of a load balancer to ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ-2025-09 in one change. Classical and TLS 1.2 clients still connect.

  3. Watch Q1 (key-exchange mix) and Q2 (classical client IPs); Q4 adds user agents through conn_trace_id. Hand the client list to app owners (language chapters).

  4. When Q3 (TLS 1.2 connections) is zero for the agreed window, move the listener to ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-3-PQ-2025-09. That ends TLS 1.2 but still allows classical TLS 1.3 key exchange.

  5. No ELB policy refuses classical TLS 1.3 key exchange. A PQ-only gate needs a server you control (Web servers and proxies).

  6. Put remaining TLS 1.2 callers on a separate load balancer or hostname and record them as exceptions.

Verify

Posture (no traffic needed):

aws elbv2 describe-load-balancers --query 'LoadBalancers[].LoadBalancerArn' --output text | tr '\t' '\n' |
while read lb; do
  aws elbv2 describe-listeners --load-balancer-arn "$lb" \
    --query 'Listeners[?Protocol==`HTTPS`||Protocol==`TLS`].[ListenerArn,Protocol,SslPolicy]' --output text
done | awk '$3 !~ /PQ-2025-09$/ {print "NOT PQ:", $0}'
aws elb describe-load-balancers --query 'LoadBalancerDescriptions[].LoadBalancerName'  # Classic: each one is a migration item
aws elbv2 describe-ssl-policies --query 'SslPolicies[?contains(Name, `PQ`)].Name'     # names in your Region

Config rule. ELBV2_PREDEFINED_SECURITY_POLICY_SSL_CHECK (elbv2-predefined-security-policy-ssl-check), resource type AWS::ElasticLoadBalancingV2::Listener, parameter sslPolicies = comma-separated PQ policy names. It covers ALB HTTPS and NLB TLS listeners. It is not available in me-central-1, ap-south-2, ap-southeast-4, ca-west-1, eu-south-2 and eu-central-2 (rule page, Documented). AWS also published a PQ-readiness conformance pack (blog, 2026-05-14) with custom ELB rules.

Live test from a container with OpenSSL 3.5 (debian:trixie-slim plus apt-get install openssl; the slim image has no openssl binary):

# PQ client must negotiate the hybrid group
echo | openssl s_client -connect app.example.com:443 -servername app.example.com -tls1_3 -groups X25519MLKEM768 2>&1 | grep 'Negotiated TLS1.3 group'
# expected: Negotiated TLS1.3 group: X25519MLKEM768
# classical client must still connect on the Res policy (fallback)
echo | openssl s_client -connect app.example.com:443 -servername app.example.com -groups X25519 2>&1 | grep -E 'Protocol|group'
# after the 1.3-only move this must fail
echo | openssl s_client -connect app.example.com:443 -servername app.example.com -tls1_2 2>&1 | grep -E 'alert|Protocol'

Observed signal in logs (field names Documented):

LogWhereFields
ALB connection log (file prefix conn_log_)S3, 5-minute files per nodetls_protocol (5), tls_cipher (6), conn_trace_id (12), tls_keyexchange (13), elb (14), ip_address (15)
ALB access logS3ssl_protocol, ssl_cipher, conn_trace_id; no key exchange
NLB access log v2.0, TLS listeners onlyS3tls_cipher (15), tls_protocol_version (16, tlsv12 or tlsv13), tls_keyexchange (17), domain_name (18)

Two documentation quirks: the NLB policy page says key exchange is "log field position 13", but the NLB field table puts tls_keyexchange 17th. AWS does not document the spelling of tls_keyexchange values, so the queries match mlkem case-insensitively. The Athena regexes were Tested against AWS's sample lines and synthetic 15- and 22-field PQ lines (python:3.13-slim, 7 of 7 parsed); Athena itself was not run.

Enforce, pitfalls and rollback

SCP (full file below). The condition key elasticloadbalancing:SecurityPolicy applies to CreateListener and ModifyListener (ALB, NLB) and to CreateLoadBalancerPolicy and SetLoadBalancerPoliciesOfListener (Classic). It does not apply to CreateLoadBalancer (ELB IAM page, Documented). This corrects the earlier version of this tab. AWS's example uses ForAnyValue:StringEquals in an Allow; the Deny below uses ForAnyValue:StringNotEquals, plus a Null check that catches HTTPS/TLS listeners created without an explicit policy (they would get ELBSecurityPolicy-2016-08). The deny logic was not tested against AWS (no credentials here): attach it to a sandbox OU first. Unverified.

SCP stages. The two allowlists in this document are stages, not a conflict. The org baseline on Enforcement and guardrails allows all eleven non-TLS-1.0 PQ ELB policies while teams migrate. The SCP below narrows that to four: ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ-2025-09, ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-3-PQ-2025-09 and their FIPS variants. Once a listener's TLS 1.2 traffic reaches zero, drop the two TLS13-1-2-Res entries so only the TLS 1.3-only PQ policies remain.

{"Sid": "DenyNonPqElbListenerPolicy", "Effect": "Deny",
 "Action": ["elasticloadbalancing:CreateListener", "elasticloadbalancing:ModifyListener"],
 "Resource": "*",
 "Condition": {"ForAnyValue:StringNotEquals": {"elasticloadbalancing:SecurityPolicy": [
   "ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ-2025-09", "ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-FIPS-PQ-2025-09",
   "ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-3-PQ-2025-09", "ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-3-FIPS-PQ-2025-09"]}}}

Pipeline. cfn-guard, OPA/conftest or Checkov custom rules on SslPolicy / ssl_policy / the LBC annotations. No built-in Checkov, tfsec or Trivy PQ rule was found. Remediation. The Config rule above, with SSM Automation calling aws elbv2 modify-listener if you allow fixes outside the pipeline.

Pitfalls

  • IaC that omits the policy gets ELBSecurityPolicy-2016-08, although the console shows a PQ default. The Load Balancer Controller behaves the same.

  • IngressClassParams.spec.sslPolicy silently overrides an Ingress ssl-policy annotation.

  • The X25519MLKEM768 key share adds about 1.2 KB, so the ClientHello often spans two packets. Old middleboxes in front of clients can drop it. Test partner networks before the 1.3-only move.

  • The backend policy follows "any listener". A search snippet claims PQ and non-PQ policies are "not compatible" on listeners of one load balancer; this is not on the policy pages read on 2026-11-14, so it is not confirmed. Move all listeners of a load balancer together anyway.

  • Policy changes are online, but clients with pinned cipher lists can fail after a 1.3-only move.

Rollback. aws elbv2 modify-listener --listener-arn ARN --ssl-policy ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-2-Res-PQ-2025-09 (from 1.3-only), or back to the previous policy. Exempt the break-glass role in the SCP so it can do this. Make the same change in IaC so the next deploy does not undo it.

configs/load-balancers/iac/lb/lb.yamliac/lb/lb.yaml (CloudFormation)
configs/load-balancers/iac/lb/scp-elb-pq.jsoniac/lb/scp-elb-pq.json

7.5 Edge and API front doors

What decides PQ here

Each managed front door terminates TLS with its own stack, so the service and the policy you pick decide the client-facing leg. CloudFront and Cognito custom domains offer PQ on TLS 1.3 already. API Gateway REST needs an opt-in SecurityPolicy_*_PQ_* policy plus an endpoint access mode. HTTP, WebSocket and edge-optimized APIs have no PQ policy. The upstream legs are classical today: CloudFront to origin, and API Gateway to its integrations. Global Accelerator does not terminate TLS, so the client and your endpoint decide.

Status matrix

Front doorDevice typeClient-facing legUpstream legDefaultEvidence
CloudFrontManaged CDNPQ on every viewer policy: X25519MLKEM768, SecP256r1MLKEM768, TLS 1.3 onlyClassical: curves prime256v1, secp384r1, X25519; OriginSSLProtocols tops out at TLSv1.2PQ, nothing to doDocumented
API Gateway REST: Regional and private APIs, custom domainsManagedOpt-in SecurityPolicy_TLS13_1_2_PQ_2025_09, SecurityPolicy_TLS13_1_2_PFS_PQ_2025_09, SecurityPolicy_TLS13_1_2_FIPS_PFS_PQ_2025_09 (TLS 1.2 and 1.3)Integration egress TLS 1.2, never PQTLS_1_0 on APIs, TLS_1_2 on domains: not PQDocumented
API Gateway REST, edge-optimizedManaged, CloudFront-frontedNo PQ policy: SecurityPolicy_TLS13_2025_EDGE, SecurityPolicy_TLS12_PFS_2025_EDGE, SecurityPolicy_TLS12_2018_EDGE, TLS_1_2TLS 1.2Not PQDocumented (policy list)
API Gateway HTTP and WebSocket APIsManagedOnly TLS_1_2; no PQ policyTLS 1.2Not PQDocumented
AppSync custom domainsManagedTLS 1.2 and 1.3; PQ not statednoneNot confirmedDocumented (versions); PQ Unverified
Cognito user pool custom domainManaged, AWS-owned CloudFront distributionTLS_V1_2_2021 and TLS_V1_3_2025 are described as post-quantum-ready; TLS_V1 discouragednoneCustomDomainConfig.SecurityPolicyDocumented
Transfer Family SFTPManagedSSH KEX mlkem768x25519-sha256, mlkem768nistp256-sha256, mlkem1024nistp384-sha384 in TransferSecurityPolicy-2025-03, TransferSecurityPolicy-FIPS-2025-03, TransferSecurityPolicy-AS2Restricted-2025-07noneOlder policies: not PQDocumented
Transfer Family FTPSManagedCipher list is ECDHE TLS 1.2 suites; no PQnoneNot PQDocumented (absence)
Transfer Family AS2ManagedNo PQ statement foundnoneNot confirmedUnverified
Global AcceleratorManaged, layer 4 (TCP/UDP)Pass-through: PQ if the client and the endpoint (ALB, NLB, EC2) negotiate it; set the policy on the endpoint, see Load balancersSame connectionFollows the endpointDocumented (layer-4 service)
Amplify Hosting, App Runner, Lightsail distributions, Verified AccessManagedNo TLS policy setting or PQ statement foundnoneNot confirmedUnverified
Lightsail load balancerManagedNo: only TLS-2016-08 and TLS-FS-1-2-Res-2019-08noneNot PQDocumented

Sources (read 2026-11-14): CloudFront viewer ciphers, CloudFront origin ciphers, API Gateway policies, Cognito custom domain, Transfer Family policies, AppSync custom domains, Lightsail TLS policies.

Client side: origin and integration legs

LegWho is the TLS clientPQ todayWhat you can doEvidence
CloudFront to custom originCloudFrontNo: X25519, P-256, P-384 only; OriginSSLProtocols max TLSv1.2Keep origins inside AWS (VPC origins, ALB in the same Region), record as an exception, watch for AWS's origin PQ releaseDocumented
CloudFront to S3 originCloudFrontNot statedTreat as classicalUnverified
API Gateway to HTTP integrationAPI GatewayNo (TLS 1.2)Private integration over VPC link to an NLB/ALB; record the hop as an exceptionDocumented (already established)
Lambda@Edge, CloudFront FunctionsNone for the viewer: they never touch the viewer handshaken/aOutbound calls from a Lambda@Edge function use its runtime: see the language chaptersUnverified (by design they run after TLS termination)
Global Accelerator to endpointThe end clientEnd to endConfigure the endpointDocumented

What origins must run: any server that offers X25519MLKEM768 will be ready when CloudFront's origin leg gains PQ, and serves PQ directly to clients that bypass CloudFront. See Web servers and proxies.

Server side: policies in IaC

Front doorTerraformCloudFormationEvidence
CloudFrontviewer_certificate.minimum_protocol_version = "TLSv1.2_2021" (PQ on TLS 1.3); "TLSv1.3_2025" drops TLS 1.2ViewerCertificate.MinimumProtocolVersionTested (validate / cfn-lint)
API Gateway REST APIaws_api_gateway_rest_api.security_policy + endpoint_access_modeAWS::ApiGateway::RestApi SecurityPolicy + EndpointAccessModeTested (validate / cfn-lint)

"Tested" here means schema-valid in Terraform 1.13.5 with aws provider 6.73.0 and in cfn-lint 1.60.1; nothing was deployed.

resource "aws_api_gateway_domain_name" "api" {
  domain_name              = "api.example.com"
  regional_certificate_arn = var.api_certificate_arn
  security_policy          = "SecurityPolicy_TLS13_1_2_PQ_2025_09"
  endpoint_access_mode     = "BASIC" # STRICT after checking access logs
  endpoint_configuration {
    types = ["REGIONAL"]
  }
}
ApiDomain:
  Type: AWS::ApiGateway::DomainName
  Properties:
    DomainName: api.example.com
    RegionalCertificateArn: !Ref ApiCertificateArn
    SecurityPolicy: SecurityPolicy_TLS13_1_2_PQ_2025_09
    EndpointAccessMode: BASIC
    EndpointConfiguration: {Types: [REGIONAL]}

Endpoint access mode. Every SecurityPolicy_* policy needs BASIC or STRICT. STRICT rejects requests whose SNI and Host do not match or that arrive from another endpoint type. Move to the PQ policy with BASIC, check access logs, then switch to STRICT. Changes take about 15 minutes (API Gateway security policies, Documented).

mTLS on API Gateway custom domains. No statement on combining mTLS with the PQ policies was found. Not confirmed; test before relying on it.

No TLS 1.3-only PQ policy on API Gateway. SecurityPolicy_TLS13_1_3_2025_09 has no PQ in its name and is not listed as PQ. To force TLS 1.3 with PQ, put CloudFront (TLSv1.3_2025) or an ALB (ELBSecurityPolicy-TLS13-1-3-PQ-2025-09) in front.

Verify

Posture:

aws apigateway get-rest-apis --query 'items[].[id,name,securityPolicy,endpointAccessMode,endpointConfiguration.types[0]]' --output table
aws apigateway get-domain-names --query 'items[].[domainName,securityPolicy,endpointAccessMode]' --output table
aws apigatewayv2 get-domain-names --query 'Items[].[DomainName,DomainNameConfigurations[0].SecurityPolicy]'   # HTTP/WebSocket: TLS_1_2 only
aws cloudfront list-distributions --query 'DistributionList.Items[].[Id,ViewerCertificate.MinimumProtocolVersion]'
aws transfer list-servers --query 'Servers[].ServerId' --output text | xargs -n1 -I{} aws transfer describe-server --server-id {} --query 'Server.[ServerId,SecurityPolicyName,Protocols]'

The securityPolicy and endpointAccessMode output field names follow the API reference naming and were not run here (no credentials). AWS's PQ-readiness conformance pack (2026-05-14) adds custom Config rules for API Gateway.

Live tests (OpenSSL 3.5 client, as in the Load balancers chapter):

echo | openssl s_client -connect api.example.com:443 -servername api.example.com -tls1_3 -groups X25519MLKEM768 2>&1 | grep 'Negotiated TLS1.3 group'
echo | openssl s_client -connect api.example.com:443 -servername api.example.com -groups X25519 2>&1 | grep Protocol   # fallback must work
sftp -o KexAlgorithms=mlkem768x25519-sha256 user@s-xxxx.server.transfer.us-east-1.amazonaws.com   # OpenSSH 9.9+

Observed signal:

ServiceFieldKey exchange logged?Evidence
CloudFront standard logs (v1, v2) and real-time logsssl-protocol, ssl-cipherNoDocumented
CloudFront origin request header CloudFront-Viewer-TLS-DetailsJSON {"tlsVersion","cipherSuite","handshakeType","keyExchange","signatureScheme","signaturePublicKeyType"}; keyExchange e.g. X25519MLKEM768Yes, if you add it to an origin request policy and log it at the originDocumented (CloudFront headers)
API Gateway access logs$context.tlsVersion, $context.cipherSuiteNoDocumented
Transfer Family SFTP logscipher and key exchangeYesDocumented

The CloudFront-Viewer-TLS-Details header replaces the earlier "third-party report" in this chapter: AWS now documents it. It can go in an origin request policy, not a cache policy. Log it at the origin, for example nginx:

log_format cf_tls '$remote_addr [$time_local] "$request" $status cf_tls=$http_cloudfront_viewer_tls_details';
access_log /var/log/nginx/cf_tls.log cf_tls;

Pitfalls, enforcement and rollback

  • STRICT endpoint access mode breaks VPC-endpoint access to a public custom domain, private APIs called without a custom domain or private DNS, and domain sharding. Use BASIC there.

  • Moving from STRICT back to BASIC makes the endpoint unavailable for about 15 minutes. Moving from an enhanced policy back to a legacy one needs the endpoint access mode set to "". An API on TLS_1_0 cannot be moved to TLS_1_2. (Documented)

  • A custom domain's policy wins over its mapped API's policy for calls through that domain. Disable or align the default execute-api endpoint, or clients can bypass the PQ policy.

  • HTTP and WebSocket APIs cannot be made PQ. Front them with CloudFront or a PQ ALB, or migrate to a Regional REST API.

  • CloudFront's origin leg and API Gateway's integration leg stay classical: record them as exceptions rather than claiming end-to-end PQ.

  • Enforcement. No IAM condition key for API Gateway or CloudFront TLS policies was found. Enforce in the pipeline (policy fields in templates) and with Config rules. Prowler has API Gateway PQ checks (its default allowlist looked wrong in review).

  • Rollback. Set the previous SecurityPolicy in IaC and deploy (about 15 minutes on API Gateway). CloudFront: set the previous MinimumProtocolVersion. Transfer Family: set the previous SecurityPolicyName.

FileWhat it is

7.6 AWS service endpoints: KMS, SSM, Secrets Manager and others

What decides PQ here

AWS owns the server side of every AWS API endpoint. On the listed services, the endpoint prefers X25519MLKEM768 whenever the client offers it ("transparent"), so the work is on the client: the SDK, the AWS CLI, or the AWS agent that makes the call. Services AWS has not listed (Systems Manager, Lambda Invoke, CloudWatch, Step Functions, ECS, EKS, the EC2 API) should be treated as classical even from a PQ client. Source: AWS PQ readiness (confidentiality), re-read 2026-11-14 (the page has no date; footer © 2026).

Status by service

Re-read 2026-11-14. "Transparent" means nothing to configure on the AWS side.

ServicePQ on the endpointCloudTrail tlsDetails.keyExchangeEvidence
KMSYes, FIPS and non-FIPS, all RegionsYesDocumented (KMS PQ TLS)
Secrets ManagerYes, FIPS and non-FIPS (not China Regions)YesDocumented (readiness, SM PQ TLS)
STSYes, FIPS and non-FIPSYesDocumented (readiness)
IAM, IAM Roles AnywhereYes, FIPS and non-FIPSYesDocumented (readiness)
ACMYes, FIPS and non-FIPSYesDocumented (readiness)
Payment CryptographyYesYesDocumented (readiness)
S3 (all bucket types)Yes, FIPS and non-FIPSNo: data events show TLS version and cipher; key exchange "coming soon"Documented (readiness)
DynamoDBNon-FIPS only; FIPS endpoints and DAX "coming soon"NoDocumented (readiness)
CodeBuild, CodePipeline, Firewall Manager, IAM Identity Center, Network Firewall (API)Yes, FIPS and non-FIPS"Coming soon"Documented (readiness)

Read "control plane only" as the management APIs. Data-plane calls (SendMessage/ReceiveMessage, Publish, PutEvents, PutRecord, image layer pulls) may still be classical. I'm not sure which ECR calls count as control plane; layer downloads go to S3-backed URLs, which are PQ-capable.

Client posture by compute type

The endpoint only negotiates PQ if the client offers it. What the client is depends on where the code runs.

Client side (app level)

AWS SDKs

From the AWS SDKs and Tools reference: Enabling hybrid post-quantum TLS (read 2026-11-14). The language chapters give the full per-runtime fix: Go, Python, Node.js, Java, .NET.

SDKPQ by default?What to doEvidence

AWS CLI

  • v2: Linux installers from 2.34.54 bundle OpenSSL 3.5.6 and prefer PQ (sdkref). Rule for pipelines: aws --version ≥ 2.34.54 on Linux. Tested: amazon/aws-cli 2.40.3 completed TLS to a PQ-only server and to a classical-only server.

  • v2 on macOS: install through Homebrew with Homebrew OpenSSL ≥ 3.5 (AWS doc). The macOS .pkg installer isn't covered by that statement; not confirmed.

  • v1: uses Python's ssl. Tested: aws-cli 1.46.3 on python:3.13-slim (Debian 13, OpenSSL 3.5.7) worked against PQ-only; on python:3.12-slim-bookworm (OpenSSL 3.0.22) it failed with SSLV3_ALERT_HANDSHAKE_FAILURE.

Parameters and Secrets Lambda Extension

  • The function calls the extension over plain HTTP on localhost:2773; there's no TLS on that hop.

  • Extension version 19 or later uses hybrid PQ TLS to Secrets Manager automatically (announcement, 2026-04-14). Documented.

  • Its Parameter Store calls go to the SSM endpoint, which isn't on the PQ list: assume classical.

Workload Credentials Provider (formerly Secrets Manager Agent) and ASCP

  • The Secrets Manager Agent was renamed AWS Workload Credentials Provider in June 2026 (docs). Version 2.0.0+ uses ML-KEM as its highest-priority group by default (SM PQ TLS).

  • AWS Secrets and Configuration Provider (ASCP, the EKS CSI provider) 2.0.0+: PQ by default (announcement). Documented.

  • The language caching libraries (Java, Python, .NET, Go) aren't named in the announcement. They use your SDK client, so the SDK row above decides.

SSM Agent, Session Manager, EC2 Instance Connect

  • The SSM Agent is Go. Its mainline go.mod says go 1.26 (go.mod, read 2026-11-14), so current builds offer X25519MLKEM768. The SSM, ssmmessages and ec2messages endpoints aren't on the PQ list, so Session Manager and Run Command traffic should be assumed classical. Unverified (needs a capture against a real endpoint).

  • EC2 Instance Connect's API is on the control-plane list. The SSH session itself is your instance's sshd (Hosts and OS).

Server side

There is nothing to configure on AWS-owned endpoints. Server-side work exists only where you own the TLS policy: ALB/NLB listeners (Load balancers); API Gateway, CloudFront, Cognito custom domains and Transfer Family (Edge and API front doors); RDS and Aurora (Databases and data services).

FIPS endpoints

KMS, Secrets Manager, STS, IAM, ACM and S3 FIPS endpoints support PQ. DynamoDB FIPS doesn't yet. Turn FIPS endpoints on per client with use_fips_endpoint = true in ~/.aws/config or AWS_USE_FIPS_ENDPOINT=true. A workload that needs both DynamoDB PQ and FIPS can't have both today.

VPC interface endpoints (PrivateLink)

An interface endpoint carries the TLS session to the same service front end, so the same rules should apply. AWS doesn't state this on the readiness page, and a third-party report says S3 through PrivateLink differs. Unverified: confirm with CloudTrail keyExchange for a KMS call made through the endpoint (vpcEndpointId is set in the record).

Packaging per compute type

ComputeWhat to shipIaC (full files below)

Verify and detect

  1. Posture: aws --version ≥ 2.34.54 (Linux). SDK versions per language tab. Extension layer version ≥ 19.

  2. Live (lab): point the client at a PQ-only test server with --endpoint-url (as done here) and at a classical-only server; both must complete TLS.

  3. Observed (production): CloudTrail tlsDetails.keyExchange for KMS, Secrets Manager, STS, IAM, Roles Anywhere, ACM and Payment Cryptography. Skip records with userIdentity.invokedBy set: AWS-service calls carry no tlsDetails (KMS doc).

CloudTrail Lake: classical calls by role and account

Not run (no AWS account here); Unverified. The keyExchange column name is assumed to match the JSON field.

SELECT recipientAccountId, eventSource, userIdentity.arn AS caller,
       tlsDetails.keyExchange AS kx, count(*) AS calls
FROM <event-data-store-id>
WHERE eventTime > timestamp '2026-11-07 00:00:00'
  AND eventSource IN ('kms.amazonaws.com','secretsmanager.amazonaws.com','sts.amazonaws.com',
                      'iam.amazonaws.com','acm.amazonaws.com','rolesanywhere.amazonaws.com',
                      'payment-cryptography.amazonaws.com')
  AND userIdentity.invokedBy IS NULL
GROUP BY 1,2,3,4
ORDER BY calls DESC

Add AND (tlsDetails.keyExchange IS NULL OR tlsDetails.keyExchange NOT LIKE '%MLKEM%') to get only the to-do list.

Athena over the CloudTrail S3 bucket

The AWS sample DDL declares tlsDetails as struct<tlsVersion,cipherSuite,clientProvidedHostHeader>. Add keyExchange:string or the column reads null. Full DDL and query in the last section. Unverified (not run).

Alert

A CloudWatch Logs metric filter on the trail's log group is AWS's documented pattern (KMS doc), extended here to all services that log keyExchange:

{ ($.eventSource = "kms.amazonaws.com" || $.eventSource = "secretsmanager.amazonaws.com" || $.eventSource = "sts.amazonaws.com" || $.eventSource = "iam.amazonaws.com" || $.eventSource = "acm.amazonaws.com") && ($.userIdentity.invokedBy NOT EXISTS) && (($.tlsDetails.keyExchange != "*MLKEM*") || ($.tlsDetails.keyExchange NOT EXISTS)) }

An EventBridge rule on "AWS API Call via CloudTrail" can do the same per call. I'm not sure EventBridge receives read-only calls such as Decrypt and GetSecretValue without extra trail settings, so the metric filter is the primary alert.

Pitfalls and rollback

  • No server-side enforcement. There's no IAM condition key for key exchange, so you can't deny classical calls by policy. Enforcement is client posture plus the alert (Enforcement and guardrails).

  • Missing tlsDetails on service-made calls is normal; don't alarm on them.

  • Unlisted services look fine and aren't. SSM, Lambda Invoke, CloudWatch and Step Functions produce no keyExchange; don't read silence as PQ.

  • Middleboxes: the hybrid ClientHello is bigger (AWS says roughly 1,600 extra handshake bytes). Egress proxies and inspection firewalls may drop it (Network devices and egress).

  • Rollback is client-side: pin the previous CLI version, or remove OPENSSL_CONF. The endpoints still accept classical clients.

7.7 Network devices, VPN and egress

What decides PQ here

A network device sets PQ only where it terminates TLS or IKE itself. A decrypting firewall, ADC or proxy runs two separate handshakes: the client leg (client to device, where the device acts as the server) and the server leg (device to origin, where the device acts as the client). Each leg gets PQ only if that device's TLS stack and profile offer ML-KEM. A device that doesn't decrypt passes the client's handshake through unchanged, so PQ then depends on the two endpoints. It only has to forward the roughly 1.2 KB larger ClientHello without dropping it. For IPsec, both IKEv2 peers must support RFC 9370 additional key exchanges with ML-KEM.

Network devices play two roles here: sensors that show which connections are classical, and converters that terminate classical TLS and re-originate PQ TLS for clients you can't upgrade. All tests on this chapter ran on 2026-11-14 in local containers, without AWS access. Scripts and outputs are in the work directory deep/netiot/ (squid/, swan/, zeek/, envoy/).

Client leg = the device acting as a TLS server toward clients. Server leg = the device acting as a TLS client toward origins.

Next-generation firewalls (TLS decryption and IPsec)

Hardware and virtual forms run the same OS, so these settings also apply to VM-Series, FortiGate-VM, CloudGuard and the other AWS Marketplace images. Check the image's OS version first: Marketplace AMIs often lag the current release.

Palo Alto PAN-OS (decryption 12.1.2+, IKEv2 11.2+)

  • Decryption, both legs (Documented): Objects > Decryption > Decryption Profile > profile > SSL Protocol Settings. Set the max version to TLSv1.3. Tick Post-quantum Cryptography (PQC) and choose PQC-Standard (ML-KEM; leave PQC-Experimental off). Under Preferred Session Settings, tick Post-Quantum SSL preferred for Client-side session (firewall as server) and/or Post-Quantum SSL preferred for Server-side session (firewall as client). It applies to SSL Forward Proxy, SSL Inbound Inspection and Decryption Mirror.

  • Cipher translation: each leg is chosen on its own, so a classical client can reach a PQ server and the reverse. A peer without PQ gets a classical handshake. The docs show no "block non-PQ" option in the decryption profile.

  • Logging: the decryption log has tls_keyxchg (Key Exchange Algorithm) and ec_curve (Elliptic Curve). Whether ec_curve shows X25519MLKEM768 is not confirmed; check on your firewall before you rely on it.

  • IKEv2 (Documented): Network > Network Profiles > IKE Crypto > profile > Advanced Options > Post-Quantum IKEv2 Additional Key Exchange: round 1 = ML-KEM-768. In IPSec Crypto > Advanced Options, set Post-Quantum IPSec Additional Key Exchange the same way (one KEM per round). On the IKE Gateway, set IKEv2 only, enable IKEv2 Fragmentation on the General chapter, and under Advanced Options > PQ KEM tick Enable Post-Quantum Key Exchange. Optionally tick Block IKEv2 if vulnerable cipher is used. Falling back to IKEv1 disables PQ.

Fortinet FortiGate (SSL inspection 8.0, IPsec 7.6.x)

  • SSL deep inspection (8.0.0+, Documented): there's no profile switch. Any policy with a deep-inspection ssl-ssh-profile negotiates X25519MLKEM768, SecP256r1MLKEM768 and SecP384r1MLKEM1024, in proxy mode and in flow mode (through the updated IPS engine). SIP-TLS isn't covered yet. Counters: diagnose ips ssl status, under "TLS 1.3 (EC)DHE Named Groups", e.g. x25519mlkem768: 1. Before 7.4.6/7.6.1, the IPS engine broke Chrome's ML-KEM handshakes in flow mode, so upgrade first.

  • IPsec (Documented): set addke1 36 in phase1-interface and phase2-interface; the full file is fortigate-ipsec-pq.conf under Full configuration files. Verify with diagnose vpn ike gateway list, which should show PQC-KEM (IKE): yes and PQC-KEM (all IPsec): yes. The PQ groups aren't available in FIPS-CC mode.

Check Point (R82, R82.10)

  • IKEv2 (Documented): SmartConsole > Object Explorer > VPN Communities > community > Encryption > tick Quantum Safe Key Exchange, then install policy. To change the profile, use the Management API: add multiple-key-exchanges, then set vpn-community-star (or -meshed) with ike-phase-1 / ike-phase-2 → use-multiple-key-exchanges true. use-standard-proposal false makes it PQ-only. It doesn't work with Traditional Mode VPN.

  • HTTPS Inspection (R82.10): adds X25519MLKEM768. A vendor blog says an inspected client that offers only PQ groups and won't accept a downgrade is dropped. The config path and log field aren't documented: not confirmed.

Cisco Secure Firewall (FTD), ASA, Juniper SRX

  • Cisco has no ML-KEM today. The roadmap puts ML-KEM IKEv2 and PQ TLS decryption in FTD 10.5 / ASA 9.25, "late 2026" (Claimed). ASA has had RFC 9370 additional key exchanges (classical) since 9.19.

  • Juniper documents only RFC 8784 post-quantum pre-shared keys (Junos Key Manager) for IPsec. A strong PPK distributed out of band protects against harvest-now attacks, but it isn't ML-KEM.

  • Interim path for these platforms: put a PQ-capable device in the path (PAN-OS or FortiGate VM, strongSwan 6.0.2+) or use SASE IPsec (Cloudflare). Record the flows that stay classical as exceptions.

Complete F5 and NetScaler files are under Full configuration files. HAProxy, nginx and other open-source server configs are on Web servers and proxies. AWS ALB and NLB PQ policies are on Load balancers.

Forward proxies and secure web gateways (egress)

Squid ssl-bump (Tested)

Squid has no TLS groups directive. http_port offers only tls-dh=[curve:]file (a single ECDH curve), and tls_outgoing_options has no curve option (http_port). Both legs therefore use the OpenSSL library defaults. Squid 6.13 on Debian 13 (squid-openssl, OpenSSL 3.5.7) negotiated X25519MLKEM768 on both legs with no Squid setting. It also bridged a classical client to a PQ-only origin, and a PQ client to a classical-only origin. Restricting the process with OPENSSL_CONF (Groups = X25519:P-256) made the PQ-only origin fail, which shows where the control point is. Squid logs the TLS version and cipher per leg (%ssl::>negotiated_version, %ssl::<negotiated_cipher) but not the group, so use Zeek or the origin's logs for that.

Test (Squid 6.13, OpenSSL 3.5.7, debian:trixie-slim)Result
PQ client → Squid → PQ-only origin200; client leg Negotiated TLS1.3 group: X25519MLKEM768
Classical client (--curves X25519) → Squid → PQ-only origin200 (Squid converts)
PQ client → Squid → classical-only origin200 (server-leg fallback)
Squid with OPENSSL_CONF Groups=X25519:P-256 → PQ-only originfails, as expected

Squid packages on Debian 12, Ubuntu 24.04 and AL2 link OpenSSL 3.0 or 1.1.1 and give no PQ. Build from a Debian 13 or Alpine 3.24 base (see Hosts and OS).

Envoy egress gateway (Tested)

Envoy 1.40.2 (BoringSSL) originated TLS 1.3 to a PQ-only origin and logged upstream_group=X25519MLKEM768. Pointed at a classical-only origin, it fell back and logged upstream_group=X25519. The group comes from the access-log fields %UPSTREAM_TLS_GROUP% and %DOWNSTREAM_TLS_GROUP%, available since 1.39.0. From 1.40.0, X25519MLKEM768 is in the default curves; setting ecdh_curves makes it explicit. Istio egress gateways are covered on Service mesh.

strongSwan test (Tested): two Alpine 3.24 containers ran strongSwan 6.0.7 with the openssl plugin (OpenSSL 3.5.9); swanctl --list-algs showed ML_KEM_512/768/1024[openssl]. The IKE SA came up as AES_GCM_16-256/PRF_HMAC_SHA2_384/CURVE_25519/KE1_ML_KEM_768. The ML-KEM share went in IKE_INTERMEDIATE (fragmented EF(1/2), EF(2/2)), and the transport-mode CHILD SA was installed. Only --cap-add NET_ADMIN was needed. The same config with Debian 13's strongSwan 6.0.1 package failed: its openssl plugin offers no ML-KEM. ML-KEM through OpenSSL 3.5 arrived in 6.0.2 (release note).

[CFG] selected proposal: IKE:AES_GCM_16_256/PRF_HMAC_SHA2_384/CURVE_25519/KE1_ML_KEM_768
[ENC] generating IKE_INTERMEDIATE request 1 [ KE ]
[ENC] parsed IKE_INTERMEDIATE response 1 [ KE ]
pq: #1, ESTABLISHED, IKEv2 ... AES_GCM_16-256/PRF_HMAC_SHA2_384/CURVE_25519/KE1_ML_KEM_768
  net: #1, reqid 1, INSTALLED, TRANSPORT, ESP:AES_GCM_16-256

AWS network services

  • AWS Network Firewall: the PQ readiness page says hybrid PQ key exchange launched on FIPS and non-FIPS endpoints. It adds that "you may need to adjust rules that aggressively drop connections", because the ClientHello is larger. The TLS inspection docs don't say which groups each leg offers, and the TLS log has no group field. Treat PQ through NFW TLS inspection as not confirmed, and test it with the PQ-only and classical-only probes under Verify. Without TLS inspection NFW passes the handshake through, so PQ is end to end. Check stateful rules that match on tls.sni or on handshake size against the 1.2 KB larger ClientHello.

  • Gateway Load Balancer: layer 3, GENEVE on UDP 6081; it never sees TLS. PQ is whatever the appliance behind it does (PAN-OS, FortiGate, Check Point as above). Allow for GENEVE overhead in the appliance MTU.

  • Transit Gateway, VPC peering, Direct Connect, PrivateLink: no TLS termination. They don't block PQ; the endpoints decide.

  • Site-to-Site and Client VPN: classical only (see VPN).

  • ALB, NLB, CloudFront, API Gateway: see Load balancers and Edge and API front doors.

Sensors: detect non-PQ connections

SensorWhat it showsHowEvidence
Zeek 7.1+ (8.0.10 tested)Negotiated group in ssl.log field curve (X25519MLKEM768, x25519, secp256r1)pq-tls.zeek adds a pq column and raises PQTLS::Classical_Key_Exchange noticesTested
Suricata 7/8Version, cipher and extension IDs only; no group—Documented (absence)

The Zeek test used a pcap of three handshakes: OpenSSL 3.5 defaults, X25519 only, and TLS 1.2. zeek-cut server_name version curve pq < ssl.log printed:

pq.example       TLSv13  X25519MLKEM768  T
classic.example  TLSv13  x25519          F
tls12.example    TLSv12  x25519          F

It also raised two notices, for example non-PQ TLS key exchange: group=x25519 version=TLSv13 sni=classic.example.

Packet capture on AWS: VPC Traffic Mirroring works only from Nitro EC2 network interfaces. It can't mirror Fargate tasks, Lambda or AWS-managed endpoints. Mirror the ENIs of NAT instances, proxies or app hosts to a Zeek sensor ENI, or to an NLB for a sensor fleet. Mirrored packets arrive VXLAN-encapsulated on UDP 4789, which Zeek decodes by default. Mirror only TCP 443 and other TLS ports to cut cost. Don't truncate packets: the hybrid ClientHello spans two segments. Terraform and CloudFormation are under Full configuration files (Traffic Mirroring).

The Squid Dockerfile and task definition are built around the tested squid.conf. They weren't deployed to AWS (no access), so treat them as a starting point.

Verify

  1. Posture: for each device, record the OS version, whether PQ is on for each leg, and the IKE proposals. Compare them with the status matrix.

  2. Client leg, through the device: from an OpenSSL 3.5 host, openssl s_client -connect <vip>:443 -groups X25519MLKEM768 -tls1_3 -brief </dev/null must print Negotiated TLS1.3 group: X25519MLKEM768. Through a forward proxy, add -proxy <proxy>:3128. A classical client (-groups X25519) must still connect; it prints Peer Temp Key: X25519, 253 bits.

  3. Server leg: point the device at a PQ-only origin (openssl s_server -groups X25519MLKEM768 -tls1_3 -www); the request must succeed. Then point it at a classical-only origin (-groups X25519); that must succeed too (fallback).

  4. IPsec: swanctl --list-sas shows KE1_ML_KEM_768. FortiOS diagnose vpn ike gateway list shows PQC-KEM (IKE): yes. The libreswan log shows addke1=ML_KEM_768.

  5. Observed signal: the Zeek ssl.log pq column, Envoy %UPSTREAM_TLS_GROUP%, HAProxy %[ssl_fc_curve], FortiOS diagnose ips ssl status. Alert on pq=F toward any endpoint you expect to be PQ.

Pitfalls and rollback

  • Middleboxes that drop big ClientHellos: X25519MLKEM768 adds about 1.2 KB, so the ClientHello spans two TCP segments. Old IPS signatures, Network Firewall rules and some load balancers dropped or reset these; FortiOS flow mode did before 7.4.6/7.6.1. Test every inline device before you turn on PQ in clients.

  • One leg PQ, the other classical: a decrypting device that is PQ on one side only gives no end-to-end PQ. Check both legs.

  • Decryption breaks pinning and mutual TLS: exclude those flows from decryption, and AWS SDK traffic to PQ endpoints such as KMS, so they stay PQ end to end.

  • IKE fragmentation: ML-KEM-768 IKE messages exceed 1,280 bytes. Enable IKEv2 fragmentation (RFC 7383) on both peers. IKE_SA_INIT can't be fragmented, which is why ML-KEM goes in IKE_INTERMEDIATE.

  • Distro packages lag: Debian 13 ships strongSwan 6.0.1 without ML-KEM (Tested). Squid, HAProxy and OpenVPN on Debian 12 and Ubuntu 24.04 link OpenSSL 3.0, which has no ML-KEM.

  • Logging gaps: Squid, F5, NetScaler and Check Point don't document a group field, and PAN-OS's is not confirmed. Add a Zeek sensor or log at the origin.

  • Rollback: decryption profiles: untick PQC (PAN-OS) or detach the cipher group (F5). IPsec: keep a classical option in the same proposal (ke1_none in strongSwan, classical proposals elsewhere) during migration, so a peer without PQ still connects. Squid: set OPENSSL_CONF to classical groups. Envoy: set ecdh_curves, or the runtime flag envoy.reloadable_features.pqc_default_ecdh_curves: false.

7.8 IoT devices

What decides PQ here

The device's TLS library decides the client side, and the broker decides the server side. On microcontrollers that library is the one compiled into the firmware: Mbed TLS (the FreeRTOS, ESP-IDF and Zephyr default) has no ML-KEM, while wolfSSL does. On Linux-class devices it is the system OpenSSL, or the s2n-tls + AWS-LC stack bundled inside the AWS CRT that the AWS IoT Device SDKs v2 use. On the server side, AWS IoT Core offers no PQ security policy, so device-to-IoT Core connections stay classical whatever the device offers. PQ is achievable today only on hops you control: a self-run broker (Mosquitto, EMQX, HiveMQ), a gateway's uplink to your own endpoints, or a PQ tunnel.

The plan therefore has three parts: ship PQ-capable stacks in new and updatable devices now, enforce PQ on brokers and gateways you run, and move IoT Core traffic when AWS adds a PQ policy. All tests on this chapter ran on 2026-11-14 in local containers, without AWS access. Scripts and outputs are in the work directory deep/netiot/ (mosq/, wolf/).

Client side (devices)

Microcontrollers: wolfSSL, the only MCU path to ML-KEM today

  • Build: wolfSSL 5.9.1+ builds ML-KEM by default. For older 5.8.x builds or user_settings.h builds, define WOLFSSL_HAVE_MLKEM, WOLFSSL_SHA3, WOLFSSL_SHAKE128, WOLFSSL_SHAKE256, HAVE_CURVE25519, WOLFSSL_TLS13 and HAVE_SUPPORTED_CURVES. Drop the sizes you don't need with WOLFSSL_NO_ML_KEM_512 / WOLFSSL_NO_ML_KEM_1024. Documented (examples/configs/user_settings_pq.h). With configure, the flag is --enable-mlkem (Claimed, wolfSSL posts).

  • Code: after wolfSSL_new(), call wolfSSL_UseKeyShare(ssl, WOLFSSL_X25519MLKEM768) so the first ClientHello carries the hybrid share. This avoids a HelloRetryRequest. Set the order with wolfSSL_CTX_set_groups() / wolfSSL_set_groups(), and keep WOLFSSL_ECC_X25519 and WOLFSSL_ECC_SECP256R1 after the hybrid group for fallback. Constants in wolfssl/ssl.h: WOLFSSL_X25519MLKEM768 = 4588, WOLFSSL_SECP256R1MLKEM768 = 4587, WOLFSSL_SECP384R1MLKEM1024 = 4589. There is no WOLFSSL_X25519_ML_KEM_768.

  • FreeRTOS coreMQTT: transport_wolfSSL.c sets no groups, so the device gets the build's defaults. Add the two calls above in TLS_FreeRTOS_Connect after wolfSSL_new (patch under Full configuration files).

  • Memory: wolfSSL reports ML-KEM-768 at about 32 KB stack and 12 KB heap with the default code. With --enable-smallstack it is about 2 KB stack and 27 KB heap. These were measured on a Raspberry Pi 5, not an MCU (Claimed, wolfSSL blog). Size TLS task stacks and receive buffers for a ClientHello of about 1.3 KB and a ServerHello of about 1.2 KB.

  • Test (Tested, wolfSSL 5.9.4, Alpine 3.24 package with WOLFSSL_HAVE_MLKEM): pqclient.c reached a PQ-only OpenSSL 3.5 server with group=X25519MLKEM768, and a classical-only server with group=X25519. The classical-only variant was refused by the PQ-only server, as expected (3/3).

Microcontrollers: Mbed TLS, ESP-IDF, Zephyr

Mbed TLS and TF-PSA-Crypto have no ML-KEM as of Mbed TLS 4.2.0 (July 2026). Trusted Firmware's minutes put ML-DSA first and "then look into ML-KEM" (Claimed). ESP-IDF esp_tls_cfg_t.groups_list takes only Mbed TLS groups. For PQ on ESP32, switch to wolfSSL with CONFIG_ESP_TLS_CUSTOM_STACK / esp_tls_register_stack(), or use wolfSSL's ESP-IDF examples. Devices that talk only to AWS IoT Core gain nothing from PQ until IoT Core supports it.

Linux-class devices and gateways: OpenSSL 3.5 and Mosquitto clients

On Debian 13 or Alpine 3.24, mosquitto_pub and mosquitto_sub offer X25519MLKEM768 first with no options. Mosquitto has no groups option. To restrict or enforce the groups, set OPENSSL_CONF for the process. Tested: mosquitto_pub reached a PQ-only broker with defaults. With OPENSSL_CONF limited to X25519/P-256 it failed with Error: Protocol error. For AL2023, Yocto and Buildroot images, see Hosts and OS.

AWS IoT Device SDK v2 (Python, JavaScript, Java, C++) via the AWS CRT

On Linux the CRT uses aws-c-io → s2n-tls + AWS-LC, bundled in the wheel, npm package or jar. Current aws-c-io maps the default to a PQ s2n policy (AWS-CRT-SDK-TLSv1.0-2025-PQ) and PQ_DEFAULT to AWS-CRT-SDK-TLSv1.2-2025-PQ (Documented, aws-c-io source).

Test (python:3.13-slim-trixie, awsiotsdk 1.31.0, awscrt 0.36.1)Result
cipher_pref = PQ_DEFAULT → PQ-only Mosquitto (mTLS)connected, published
cipher_pref = DEFAULT → PQ-only Mosquittoconnected (the default is already PQ on Linux)
cipher_pref = TLSv1_2_2025_07 (non-PQ) → PQ-only MosquittoAWS_IO_TLS_ERROR_NEGOTIATION_FAILURE, as expected
PQ_DEFAULT → normal Mosquitto (classical-capable)connected

To make PQ explicit in Python, set opts.cipher_pref = awscrt.io.TlsCipherPref.PQ_DEFAULT before ClientTlsContext(opts). In C++ the same enum value is AWS_IO_TLS_CIPHER_PREF_PQ_DEFAULT on Aws::Crt::Io::TlsContextOptions (setter name not verified). JS, Java and C++ weren't tested, but they use the same aws-c-io. Older awscrt versions have only PQ_TLSv1_0_2021_05, which is Kyber round 3 and deprecated: upgrade instead of selecting it. PKCS#11/TPM keys: aws-c-io uses legacy TLS 1.2 ELB policies for custom key operations and refuses TLS 1.3, so those devices get no PQ. Against IoT Core, the hybrid offer simply falls back to classical.

Greengrass v2, Android, OPC UA, LoRaWAN, cellular

  • Greengrass nucleus (Java): MQTT goes through aws-crt-java (0.45.0 in the current pom), so PQ is likely offered on Linux. This is inferred, not confirmed, and IoT Core is classical anyway. Component HTTPS calls follow the JDK (Java). Nucleus lite links the system OpenSSL, so use an OpenSSL 3.5 OS image (Debian 13, Alpine 3.24, or Yocto with OpenSSL 3.5).

  • Android gateways: whether Conscrypt has X25519MLKEM768 on by default per Android version is not confirmed; see Mobile, desktop and CLI clients.

  • OPC UA: no PQ SecurityPolicy exists (OPC 10000-2, 1.05.06). Over untrusted links, carry OPC UA in a PQ tunnel: strongSwan 6.0.2+ IPsec or a PQ TLS proxy (Network devices and egress).

  • LoRaWAN: AES-128 session keys and no public-key handshake, so ML-KEM doesn't apply on the radio link. The network server's cloud backhaul is ordinary TLS; treat it like a Linux gateway. No LoRa Alliance PQ statement found.

  • Cellular: 3GPP's PQ transition study is TR 33.703 (Release 20). There is no ML-KEM in 5G NAS/AS yet, so protect the IP payload with TLS or IPsec on top.

Server side (brokers and endpoints)

EndpointPQ settingEvidence
AWS IoT Core (default and custom domains)None. IoT Core isn't on AWS's PQ readiness pageDocumented

EMQX 5.x / 6.xErlang ssl option {supported_groups, [x25519mlkem768, x25519, secp256r1]} exists from ssl 11.5 (OTP 28.4 timeframe). EMQX documents no key for it, so PQ follows the bundled OTP default (preferred from ssl 11.7). Check which OTP your EMQX release bundlesDocumented (OTP) / Not found (EMQX key)
HiveMQRuns on the JVM, so PQ follows the JDK (see Java for which builds default to X25519MLKEM768, or the BC JSSE agent). HiveMQ documents no named-group setting; the standard JSSE property is -Djdk.tls.namedGroups=X25519MLKEM768,x25519,secp256r1 in JAVA_OPTSNot found (HiveMQ)
Broker behind an NLBTCP passthrough keeps the broker's TLS (PQ as above). A TLS listener terminates at the NLB, so use a PQ policy there (Load balancers)Documented (ELB)

Packaging per compute type

The Dockerfiles and the drop-in are built around the tested configs. They weren't deployed to AWS (no access).

Verify

  1. Inventory: for each device model, record the TLS library and version and whether OTA is possible. Store them as thing attributes (for example tlsLib=wolfssl-5.9.4), then query fleet indexing for anything below wolfSSL 5.9.1, OpenSSL 3.5 or a current awscrt.

  2. Broker, PQ-only client must succeed: openssl s_client -connect broker:8883 -groups X25519MLKEM768 -tls1_3 -cert dev.pem -key dev.key -CAfile ca.pem -brief </dev/null prints Negotiated TLS1.3 group: X25519MLKEM768.

  3. Broker fallback: the same command with -groups X25519 must still connect, unless you enforce PQ.

  4. Device as client: point a test device at openssl s_server -accept 8883 -groups X25519MLKEM768 -tls1_3 -cert srv.pem -key srv.key -CAfile ca.pem -Verify 1. It must complete the handshake. Against -groups X25519 it must fall back.

  5. Observed signal: the broker log (no suitable key share means a classical client was refused in PQ-only mode), a Zeek ssl.log curve on a mirrored gateway uplink (Network devices and egress), or wolfSSL_get_curve_name() / s2n_connection_get_kem_group_name() logged on the device.

Pitfalls and rollback

  • Certificates: AWS IoT Core documents RSA and ECDSA device certificates only; no ML-DSA support was found (checked 2026-11-16). Devices that ship a trust root in firmware keep it for their whole life, so new firmware should carry an ML-DSA root next to the ECDSA root and use a TLS stack that can verify ML-DSA (OpenSSL 3.5, wolfSSL 5.8+). Firmware signing can use ML-DSA from AWS KMS today (Documented). See Use cases in the overview.

  • IoT Core is classical: don't hold device rollout back waiting for PQ on IoT Core. Ship PQ-capable stacks now, and enforce PQ on your own brokers and gateways first. Watch the IoT Core security policies page for a PQ policy. Device lifetimes of 10 years or more make procurement the cheapest point to fix this.

  • Buffers and stacks: a hybrid ClientHello is about 1.3 KB and the ServerHello about 1.2 KB. MCU TLS receive buffers of 1–2 KB, MQTT_NETWORK_BUFFER_SIZE and task stacks need headroom. Test on real hardware for heap fragmentation, handshake time and battery cost.

  • HelloRetryRequest: if the device lists the hybrid group but sends only an X25519 share, a PQ-only server forces an extra round trip. Send the hybrid key share first (wolfSSL_UseKeyShare).

  • PKCS#11 and secure-element keys: the CRT path gives no TLS 1.3 and so no PQ there. The alternative is wolfSSL with a crypto callback for the secure element.

  • Stale names: PQ_TLSv1_0_2021_05 (awscrt) and the Kyber IDs such as WOLFSSL_P521_KYBER_LEVEL5 are pre-standard, and servers no longer accept them. Replace them.

  • ESP-IDF registry lag: the managed wolfSSL component is 5.8.2, older than default-on ML-KEM. Pin a newer wolfSSL, or enable ML-KEM in user_settings.h, and test.

  • Rollback: brokers: remove the OPENSSL_CONF drop-in to return to hybrid-preferred with classical fallback. Devices: keep X25519/P-256 after the hybrid group so that fallback needs no change, and ship an OTA config flag that skips wolfSSL_UseKeyShare if a model misbehaves.

7.9 Service mesh and east-west traffic

Linkerd 2.19+ is PQ for meshed traffic by default. Istio 1.27+ and Consul 2.1+ need one setting, and Istio's setting enforces PQ-only. Cilium's node encryption, ECS Service Connect and Kuma have no PQ key-exchange setting that we could find. App Mesh was shut down on 2026-09-30. Envoy configs and Kyverno guardrails were tested locally on 2026-11-14; no cluster was stood up.

What decides PQ here

The mesh proxy's TLS library and the group list the control plane pushes to it. The app talks plaintext to its local proxy, and proxy-to-proxy mTLS carries the hop: Envoy (BoringSSL) in Istio sidecars, Istio gateways, Consul and ECS Service Connect; the Rust proxies (Linkerd proxy, Istio ztunnel) use aws-lc. If the app does its own TLS, the mesh only forwards the encrypted bytes and the app's runtime decides (language chapters). Gateways and TLS origination to external hosts are "non-mesh TLS" and often have a separate setting. Certificate signatures stay classical in every mesh listed (Certificates (ML-DSA) chapter).

Status matrix

Mesh and versionData pathPQ by defaultHow to turn onComputeEvidence
Linkerd 2.19+ (from edge-25.8.4)Meshed pod-to-pod mTLSYes, prefers X25519MLKEM768Upgrade control plane, restart workloadsEKSDocumented (2.19 announcement, edge-25.8.4)
Istio 1.27+ sidecarEnvoy-to-Envoy mTLS, gateway downstream and upstream, xDSNoCOMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc on istiod: TLS 1.3 and X25519MLKEM768 onlyEKSDocumented (1.27 change notes); Helm render Tested
Istio 1.27+ ambientztunnel HBONE mTLS, waypointsNoCOMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc on istiod and ztunnelEKSDocumented; Helm render Tested

Lambda functions are not meshed; their outbound TLS is the language tabs.

Client side (app level)

Inside a mesh, the best client setting is none: send plaintext HTTP to the local proxy and let the mesh do mTLS. Then PQ follows the mesh row above, whatever language the app uses.

  • Calls to services inside the mesh: use http://svc.ns.svc.cluster.local. If the app uses https:// itself, the mesh only passes the bytes through, and the app's runtime picks the groups (Go, Python, Node.js, Java, .NET chapters).

  • Calls to external hosts (egress): either the app does TLS (language chapter), or the app sends plain HTTP and Istio originates TLS with a ServiceEntry plus a DestinationRule in tls.mode: SIMPLE. Origination is non-mesh TLS, so it follows meshConfig.tlsDefaults.ecdhCurves or COMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc.

    trafficPolicy:
  portLevelSettings:
  - port: { number: 443 }
    tls: { mode: SIMPLE, sni: api.partner.example, credentialName: partner-ca }

  • Linkerd: traffic to unmeshed or external destinations is not mesh mTLS; PQ there is the app's own TLS.

  • ECS without a PQ mesh: run a self-managed Envoy sidecar; the app calls http://127.0.0.1:8080/<service>/ and Envoy originates TLS with ecdh_curves (task definition and Dockerfile in Full configuration files; Envoy config in the Web servers chapter).

Server side (mesh control plane and proxies)

  • Istio (enforcing, all proxy TLS): istiod Helm values pilot.env.COMPLIANCE_POLICY: pqc; ambient adds the ztunnel chart value env.COMPLIANCE_POLICY: pqc. Restart workloads so proxies reconnect.

    pilot:
  env:
    COMPLIANCE_POLICY: pqc

  • Istio (gateways only, with fallback): meshConfig.tlsDefaults.ecdhCurves: [X25519MLKEM768, X25519]. Use this for internet-facing gateways, because COMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc also covers gateway downstream TLS and would refuse classical clients.

  • Linkerd 2.19+: nothing to set. helm upgrade linkerd-crds then linkerd-control-plane, then kubectl rollout restart each meshed workload.

  • Consul 2.1+: mesh config entry and agent TLS:

    Kind = "mesh"
TLS {
  Outgoing {
    TLSMinVersion = "TLSv1_3"
    TLSMaxVersion = "TLSv1_3"
    ECDHCurves    = ["X25519MLKEM768", "X25519"]
  }
}

    Incoming takes the same three keys. Agent tls.defaults: tls_min_version = "TLSv1_3" and tls_ecdh_curves = "X25519MLKEM768,X25519" (a comma-separated string, not a list). Allowed names: X25519MLKEM768, X25519, P-256, P-384, P-521.

  • Envoy self-managed: ecdh_curves: ["X25519MLKEM768", "X25519"] in both DownstreamTlsContext and UpstreamTlsContext (Tested; full file in the Web servers chapter).

  • Cilium: keep IPsec per-tunnel keys (3+ prefix; global keys deprecated in 1.16) or WireGuard; neither gives PQ key exchange, so put PQ in a mesh layer above it when the threat model needs it.

Guardrails (Kyverno). The earlier ClusterPolicy version had three checks, run in Audit first: istiod and ztunnel must carry COMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc; Envoy Gateway ClientTrafficPolicy.spec.tls.ecdhCurves must start with X25519MLKEM768; the ingress-nginx ConfigMap ssl-ecdh-curve must start with X25519MLKEM768. It passed on Kyverno CLI v1.16.0 (3 compliant resources passed, 3 non-compliant failed), but Kyverno 1.20 no longer runs kyverno.io/v1 ClusterPolicy (Tested: the 1.20 CLI rejects the file). Only the istiod check has been ported to the CEL ValidatingPolicy shown here (Tested, CLI 1.20.0); the ztunnel and Envoy Gateway checks are not ported yet, so on 1.20+ write them as ValidatingPolicy before relying on them. Gatekeeper equivalents were not written. Org-wide controls are in the Enforcement and guardrails tab.

Packaging per compute type

  • EKS (Istio): helm upgrade istiod istio/istiod -n istio-system -f values-istiod-pqc.yaml; ambient also helm upgrade ztunnel istio/ztunnel -n istio-system -f values-ztunnel-pqc.yaml. Rendering the 1.28.0 charts put COMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc on the istiod container and tlsDefaults.ecdhCurves in the istio ConfigMap (Tested, helm template only). IstioOperator form is in Full configuration files.

  • EKS (Linkerd): chart version 2.19+ (or Buoyant Enterprise 2.19+); no values.

  • ECS / Fargate: Service Connect has no group setting, so for PQ between ECS services today use either app-level TLS (language chapters) or a self-managed Envoy sidecar: Dockerfile plus task definition in Terraform and CloudFormation below (CloudFormation passed cfn-lint; not deployed).

  • EC2 (Consul): agent tls.hcl in /etc/consul.d/, mesh entry with consul config write mesh.hcl, then restart the agent and Envoy sidecars.

Verify

  1. Posture. Istio: istioctl version 1.27+; kubectl -n istio-system get deploy istiod -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.containers[0].env}' | grep COMPLIANCE_POLICY. Linkerd: linkerd version 2.19+ and linkerd check --proxy (all proxies on the new version). Consul: consul version 2.1+ and consul config read -kind mesh -name mesh.

  2. Envoy counters (Istio, Consul, self-managed). On fresh connections, ssl.curves.X25519MLKEM768 must rise and ssl.curves.X25519 must not. Tested on standalone Envoy 1.36: listener.0.0.0.0_8443.ssl.curves.X25519MLKEM768: 5 and cluster.be_pq.ssl.curves.X25519MLKEM768: 1.

    kubectl exec deploy/orders -c istio-proxy -- pilot-agent request GET 'stats?filter=ssl.curves'
kubectl exec deploy/orders -c istio-proxy -- pilot-agent request GET config_dump | grep -A3 ecdh_curves

  3. Linkerd. 2.19 exports the negotiated TLS key exchange as proxy metrics (announcement); check linkerd diagnostics proxy-metrics -n <ns> deploy/<name> for the key-exchange label. The exact metric name was not confirmed here.

  4. Gateways, live. From outside: openssl s_client -connect gw:443 -tls1_3 -groups X25519MLKEM768 must show Negotiated TLS1.3 group: X25519MLKEM768; -groups X25519 must still connect unless you chose COMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc, which refuses it.

  5. Envoy test results (2026-11-14). Envoy 1.36.13 and Istio's Envoy 1.36.3 with the Web servers chapter config: 12 of 12 checks passed (PQ client, classical fallback, TLS 1.2 refused, PQ-only and classical backends reached). With ecdh_curves removed, the PQ-only client was refused and the PQ-only backend gave 503 (expected).

Pitfalls and rollback

  • COMPLIANCE_POLICY=pqc is PQ-only. It refuses peers without X25519MLKEM768: older Istio in multicluster, classical clients on ingress gateways, and external hosts reached by TLS origination. Turn it on per cluster after every peer is 1.27+; use tlsDefaults.ecdhCurves with fallback for internet-facing gateways.

  • tlsDefaults.ecdhCurves does not touch mesh mTLS and applies mesh-wide; there is no per-workload setting (Red Hat OSSM docs).

  • FIPS builds. Red Hat says PQC is unavailable on OpenShift clusters in FIPS mode. Envoy BoringSSL-FIPS before 1.40 defaults to P-256 only (Envoy's 1.40.0 changelog adds the hybrid to FIPS builds' defaults too, not tested here); Consul lists FIPS Envoy builds 1.38.4 and 1.39.1 that accept the hybrid group. Check your FIPS image before turning it on.

  • Envoy upstream maximum TLS defaults to 1.2: a TLS 1.3 minimum without a maximum fails with NO_SUPPORTED_VERSIONS_ENABLED (Tested). Consul notes the same.

  • Existing connections keep their groups until they close. Restart workloads or wait for connection churn before measuring.

  • Mixed versions (Linkerd proxies older than 2.19, Envoys without ML-KEM) negotiate classical silently with Linkerd and Consul fallback lists.

  • Apps doing their own TLS bypass all of this.

  • Rollback. Istio: remove COMPLIANCE_POLICY (and tlsDefaults.ecdhCurves), helm upgrade, restart istiod, ztunnel and workloads. Consul: set ECDHCurves = ["X25519"] and write the mesh entry. Linkerd: no switch to turn PQ off was found; downgrading below 2.19 is the only route (not recommended).

Configuration files

The complete files for this chapter are in the repository under configs/service-mesh/. This paper shows only the main change from each; copy from the repository, not from the paper. 6 files:

FileWhat it is
configs/service-mesh/values-istiod-pqc.yamlvalues-istiod-pqc.yaml, values-ztunnel-pqc.yaml, istio-operator-pqc.yaml, egress-tls-origination.yaml (Istio)
configs/service-mesh/mesh.hclmesh.hcl, mesh.yaml, /etc/consul.d/tls.hcl, systemd drop-in (Consul)
configs/service-mesh/DockerfileDockerfile (self-managed Envoy sidecar for ECS; envoy.yaml is in the Web servers chapter)

7.10 Databases and data services

What decides PQ here

Two TLS stacks meet on every database connection. The server engine (its version, its group setting and the TLS library it links) decides which groups it accepts; the client driver's TLS stack decides which groups it offers. Hybrid X25519MLKEM768 needs both sides to support it, TLS 1.3, and no setting on either side that pins a classical list.

On managed services AWS owns the server stack, so you change a parameter group or the engine version. On self-managed engines you choose the image and set the engine's group option, or an OpenSSL config file where the engine has none. Tests in this chapter ran on 2026-11-14 (arm64 Docker); scripts and output are kept in the data work directory.

Status matrix

Managed AWS data services

ServicePQ (server side)Minimum version / settingEvidence

Client side (app level)

The driver's TLS stack decides what the client offers. Runtime rules live in the language chapters: Go, Python, Node.js, Java, .NET. Database-specific points:
